# Mathematics manuscript collection

**719 manuscripts covering 26 result families.**

[**Read the overview PDF**](overview.pdf).

## Manuscript map

Each result description is followed by its constituent manuscripts and their abstracts. Paper titles link directly to PDFs.

<table>
<thead><tr><th>Result</th></tr></thead>
<tbody><tr>
<td>

**001. Packings of lines in R10.** First values for 6 blank cells (N = 101, 103, 106, 109, 119–120) in the row m = 10, n = 1 of the table of best Grassmannian packings, blank in Sloane's 1997 table and in Cohn's current one. Each value beats every packing obtained by deleting subspaces from a larger known one.

</td>
</tr></tbody>
<tbody><tr>
<td>

&emsp;[A Packing of 101 Lines in R10](preprints/A-Packing-of-101-Lines-in-R10-October-9-2026/paper.pdf)

We give 101 1-dimensional subspaces of R^10 whose smallest squared chordal distance is at least 0.781944. This cell of the table of best Grassmannian packings has no published value. The best value implied by known packings is 0.780393, obtained by deleting 1 of the 102 lines of the packing found by this search. The value is checked with exact rational arithmetic from integer coordinates. We do not claim it is optimal.

</td>
</tr></tbody>
<tbody><tr>
<td>

The value is checked with exact rational arithmetic from integer coordinates. We do not claim it is optimal.

The best value implied by known packings is 1.748962, obtained by deleting 1 of the 67 planes of the packing found by this search. The value is checked with exact rational arithmetic from integer coordinates. We do not claim it is optimal.

The best value implied by known packings is 2.941951, obtained by deleting 1 of the 17 four-spaces of the packing found by this search. The value is checked with exact rational arithmetic from integer coordinates. We do not claim it is optimal.

<td>

**026. Spreading points in the plane.** First values for n = 32–50 (19 cells) in Erich Friedman's table of point sets in the plane minimizing the ratio of the largest to the smallest distance, which stops at n = 31. Each value is smaller than what deleting a point from the next configuration gives.

</td>
</tr></tbody>
<tbody><tr>
<td>

&emsp;[Spreading 32 Points in the Plane](preprints/Spreading-32-Points-in-the-Plane-October-8-2026/paper.pdf)

We place 32 points in the plane so that the squared ratio of the largest to the smallest distance between them is at most 29.22375+. Erich Friedman's table of best known configurations lists n up to 31. Deleting a point from our 33-point configuration gives 29.80601; this one is smaller. The ratio is computed exactly from the published coordinates. We do not claim it is optimal.

</td>
</tr></tbody>
